Related to `KB-91`: the graph moved to the left edge to stop covering the speedometer, and NCM's own toasts render at `middle_left`, so two NCM surfaces now compete for that edge. **Do not guess a new position** -- that is how the odometer landed on the health bar (KB-90). Wait for the owner to say where, against a real screenshot. |

The player-facing identity of a machine is its **serials** (`NCM #0000018` / `HELLA #0004`), its name, and its provenance. Replace the UUID line with named serials; keep the UUID only where a developer needs it, if anywhere. Together with `IDEA-316` this makes both surfaces say the same kind of thing in the same words. |
| IDEA-319 | **F0** | 2026-09-24 | **Serial order does not follow creation order when bootstrap stock is backfilled after production has already minted.** On NCM Development the three founding market machines (asset UUIDs `...0001/0002/0003`) carry the world's **highest** ordinals -- NCM #16/#17/#18, and the LAST model ordinal in each line (MaiMai 8/8, Galena 6/6, Hella 4/4) -- despite being the first machines that existed. | found verifying KB-96, 2026-09-24 | **FIXED 2026-09-24 (code); fresh-world live PASS OWED** | provenance.lua backfill; `IDEA-226` | Cause: production mints **take an ordinal at mint time**, while seeded/bootstrap assets have `production_ordinal IS NULL` and only receive one when `NcmProvenance.backfill` runs. On Online the backfill ran before any production existed, so the founding MaiMai is correctly **NCM #0000001**. On Development every migration applied in one boot and production ticked before the backfill, so the founding stock was numbered after machines built later. **Harmless on a disposable world; on any future fresh world it would permanently mis-order the founding machines' place in history**, which is precisely what `IDEA-226`'s three immutable sequences exist to get right. Fix is ordering, not renumbering: backfill before the first production tick, or seed with ordinals already allocated. **FIX:** run `NcmProvenance.backfill` a second time immediately after `NcmMarket.seed` and before `NcmMarket.start`, so founding stock is numbered before the first production tick can take ordinals. Idempotent -- backfill touches only `production_ordinal IS NULL` -- so it costs one no-op query on every later boot. **It renumbers nothing:** ordinals are append-only and immutable (`IDEA-226`/L119), so Development keeps NCM #16/#17/#18 and Online keeps its correct #1. This fixes the next fresh world, not the past. **Verification owed:** the no-op path is confirmed (restart 2026-09-24, zero `[ERR]`, no numbering line because nothing was NULL), but **the fix itself can only be proven on a genuinely fresh world** -- by definition the bug needs one. Not claimed as passed. |

The write site now carries the position: it uses one the caller already validated where available, otherwise reads the authoritative snapshot **respecting `fresh`** -- a stale snapshot is a position the driver has already left, and a wrong coordinate is worse than none. **One bug caught during the build:** three unknown coordinates would have passed `nil` into the MySQL params array, silently truncating the value list and failing the whole insert -- the trap that already cost this exact function a live identity-reveal outage (SQL 1292). A naive counter cannot tell an hour in the passenger seat from thirty cars in a car park. **The dwell timer already measures duration and then discards it** -- same shape as `IDEA-236` and `IDEA-329`: the value is in hand at the moment it is thrown away, and cannot be reconstructed afterwards. Thirty encounters in one minute is one experience, not thirty. |

Then 2-3 seconds later `ncm_world` teleports them again to their stored location -- so the player sees a death, a respawn somewhere they did not choose, and a second fade. **The premise is also out of date:** `Open77.players.teleport` exists in `.87` and NCM uses it, which is why NCM's own placement costs no death. | IDEA-421 | **F0** | 2026-09-25 | **First force-feedback report from hardware nobody here owns, and it found four defects in one sitting.** monki, on a Thrustmaster **T128**, tested each channel in isolation with the others zeroed -- the method the project had been asking for and had never had. | monki, 2026-09-25 | **FOUR FIXED AND REGRESSION-COVERED; the engine frequency is owed an owner decision** | `KB-103`-`KB-106`; `IDEA-422`; `test_resume.py` | Verbatim, because the wording is diagnostic: *"After armed Force Input I can Test Pulse the wheel and get a feedback but the button works only once. To make the Test Pulse work again I have to press unarm and arm"*; *"Engine HUE makes the whole wheel vibrate and whine... it feels like an electric razor"*; *"Stiffness... very grainy, like having sand in the gears"*; *"Centering is kinda working... but after a couple seconds my wheel isn't getting FFB anymore"*; *"Road even at 100% the output is almost 0"*; *"the slider refused multiple times"*. **What worked:** device detection, F6 panel, steering range, FF strength across every rung, and the Market -- he bought a Galena. **Two of his six symptoms were one bug** (`KB-103`), which is only visible once the reports are laid beside each other. The report is the reason four defects were found in an afternoon and the reason none of them could have been found by reading the source: every one of them is silent, error-free and looks correct. |
| IDEA-422 | **F1** | 2026-09-25 | **The engine channel is a fixed 125 Hz buzz that does not track engine speed at all** -- only its amplitude moves. On a heavy gear-drive G29 that reads as texture; on a lighter belt wheel it is a whine. | monki, 2026-09-25: *"it's definitely vibrating too fast, I guess something like 100x faster, it feels like an electric razor"* | **DECIDED AND BUILT 2026-09-25 (owner: follow the rpm); live PASS owed** | `IDEA-421`; `ncm-wheel.py` `engine_period_ms`; `test_resume.py` | `e.periodic.period = 8` is 125 Hz, a constant, carrying the comment *"125 Hz: texture. 25 Hz was swallowed whole"* -- which is an honest record of tuning by feel on **one** wheel and is exactly the class of number `IDEA-411` already caught once. **Deliberately not fixed blind.** Three options and they are genuinely different products: a fixed LOW rumble (simple, safe, wrong-feeling to a sim driver); period derived from rpm so the wheel rises and falls with the engine (right, and the reason a wheel beats a gamepad); or per-device-class frequency (acknowledges that the same number cannot serve a G29 and a T128). **Guessing a second time on a wheel I cannot hold is the mistake `IDEA-411` was logged to prevent.** **Owner chose rpm-linked.** Built as a named band -- `ENGINE_HZ_IDLE = 12`, `ENGINE_HZ_SPAN = 35` -- giving 16 Hz at idle, 27 Hz cruising, 48 Hz at redline, against a constant 125 Hz before. **Deliberately not the true firing frequency**, which for a four-cylinder four-stroke is rpm/30 and reaches 233 Hz at redline: reproducing it faithfully lands straight back in razor territory at the top, which is the complaint. What hands read as an engine is the sub-harmonic. Amplitude still tracks load as before, so this changes how FAST the buzz is, not how strong. An unmeasured rev ratio keeps the previous period rather than falling to idle -- absence is not zero (`IDEA-361`). The resulting Hz is reported in `describe()` so the next complaint arrives with a number instead of a simile. **Writing the test found a latent crash**: NaN survives `float()` and defeats every comparison, so a NaN rev ratio reached `int()` and raised inside `write()`, whose handler silences the wheel -- the same NaN rule `Knowledge.reading` already enforces on the Lua side, now enforced here. 18 assertions. |

Static front/rear split reads ~60/40 for a front-engined car, and the resting residual against gravity is `-0.0`. | Carrier 0.1.4-0.1.9 live drives | **validated** | `IDEA-495`; MASTER_TEST §AU | Per-corner *normal load* is therefore addressable. | IDEA-503 | **ENG** | 2026-09-26 | **`insert1` -- the only surviving route to per-corner grip, bookmarked with the exact checks and the exact leverage we now hold.** §AV2 closed the cheap route: scaling the four per-corner normal-load force entries by plus or minus 20 percent, zero-sum, changed lateral capability by under 5 percent, which is nothing. What is left is the per-wheel data on the live physics instance itself. Report 36 §3.4 declares `vehicle::WheeledPhysics : Physics` carrying `physics::VehiclePhysicsInsert2 insert2[4]`, named *WheelRuntimeData*, beside `slipAngleCurveScale`, `brakingFrictionFactor`, `wheelsFrictionMap` and the weight-transfer pair. **`insert1` is shorthand, not a verified symbol** -- it is the name used here for the per-wheel *configuration* array that would sit beside the runtime one and hold `frictionMulLateral` / `frictionMulLongitudinal`. Confirming the symbol exists, and what it declares, is check zero and costs one file read. | MASTER_TEST §AV2 negative; Report 36 §3.4; `physics_probe.cpp` | **BOOKMARKED -- not scheduled. Parts proceeds without it (`IDEA-505`).** | Parts tyres/suspension/brakes/aero; an `L148` successor if a write is ever authorised | **WHAT TO CHECK, in order, cheapest and safest first.** **(0) Confirm the symbol.** Read `RED4ext/Scripting/Natives/vehiclePhysics.hpp` in the Let There Be Flight SDK fork. Establish whether `insert1` exists, what it holds and its declared offset. If there is no such array, per-corner grip moves entirely to `wheelsFrictionMap` and the slip-curve scalars, which are vehicle-level -- and §AV2 becomes the final word on per-corner grip rather than a partial one. **(1) Locate `insert2[4]` on 2.31 by correlation, never by trusting a declared offset.** The header's offsets are from a 1.52-1.61 map, and Direct Wheel found `maxWheelTurnDeg` at `0x1A4` on 2.31 where the header says `0xC10`. **The names carry forward; the offsets do not.** But we hold something Report 36 did not: a live pointer to this exact struct at 60 Hz, plus four *already-identified* per-corner normal loads. Sweep the struct for four-float groups that track our classified wheel entries corner by corner and `insert2` identifies itself. LTBF's shipped `GetDampedSpringForce(wheelIndex)` reads out of that array, so a damped spring force is the quantity to expect. **(2) Calibrate the whole header from that one anchor.** Once `insert2` is placed on 2.31, every other `WheeledPhysics` field is a fixed delta from it, `insert1` included. That turns guessing into arithmetic and is the highest-value step in this list. **(3) Read before writing, for a full session.** Log the candidate friction fields on the tracked body and confirm they are constant per car, differ between cars and change on respawn. A field that drifts frame to frame is derived state and must not be written -- `insert2` being named *WheelRuntimeData* is an explicit warning that part of this region is output rather than configuration. **§AV2 is precisely the mistake this step prevents:** three drives spent writing something that turned out to sit downstream of the model. **(4) Only then: one field, one corner, bounded, inert by default, under a new decision line.** `L148` does not cover it and must not be stretched to. **WHAT ARMS US, that did not exist when Report 36 called this UNPROVEN.** **(a)** A verified live pointer to the vehicle's own physics data every frame from `RigidBody::Recalculate` (hash `593628708`), player's car latched, traffic excluded. **(b)** Two verified landmarks inside that struct on 2.31 -- velocity at `+0x18`, matched against NCM's own speedometer across 0-25 m/s, and mass at `+0x1BC` reading 1100 kg stably. **`total_mass` is a named `vehDriveModelData` flat**, so `+0x1BC` is plausibly a named field rather than an anonymous float, which makes it a second calibration point and not merely a curiosity. **(c)** A shipped precedent 12 bytes away: LTBF writes `physicsData->unk1B0` for `EnableGravity`. Another released mod writes this struct, in this region, and holds. **(d)** Proven corner identity -- FL, FR, RR, RL, a ring rather than row-major -- from three independent manoeuvres across two cars. That is the correlation key for step 1 and it was expensive to obtain. **(e)** An SEH-guarded read/write harness with pointer-span caching, fault latching that disables writes for the session and says so, and a hot-reloading config, all proven at 60 fps on Production. **(f)** A ground-truth oracle: the four corner loads sum to the negation of gravity with the residual pinned near zero. Any write that desynchronises the solver shows in that residual immediately, with nobody guessing -- it is what confirmed the §AV2 write was arithmetically correct even though it did nothing. **(g)** An address-table pre-check that makes a patch-day miss a log line instead of a terminated process. **WHAT EACH ANSWER BUYS.** A positive makes tyres, suspension, brakes and aero express *asymmetrically* -- front/rear balance, brake bias, anti-roll, per-corner tyre temperature, downforce-as-grip. A negative means every physical Part family collapses to vehicle-level magnitude plus NCM simulation: a smaller but entirely shippable product, and the one `IDEA-505` instructs Parts to assume. <br><br>**CHECK 0 EXECUTED THE SAME DAY -- the SDK header was already on disk, so it cost one read. Three findings, and the second one redirects the plan.** **(1) `insert1` IS REAL.** `physics::VehiclePhysicsInsert1 insert1[4]` with `insert1Count`, declared in its own `vehiclePhysicsInsert1.hpp`. On the 1.52/1.6 map it spans `0x110` to `0x5C0` -- **300 bytes per wheel** -- with `insert1Count` at `0x5C0` and `RED4EXT_ASSERT_OFFSET(WheeledPhysics, insert2[0], 0x5E0)` immediately after. The shorthand was right. **(2) IT IS NOT ON THE OBJECT WE HOLD, AND STEP 1 AS ORIGINALLY WRITTEN WOULD HAVE FAILED.** `insert1` lives on `WheeledPhysics`. Our detour receives `vehiclePhysicsData`, and the header proves `Physics` and `PhysicsData` are **separate objects**: `Physics::sub_50` *"sets this->velocity from physicsData->velocity"*, and the commented-out `GetPhysicsData()` carries the pattern `48 8B 49 60` -- `mov rcx,[rcx+0x60]` -- so `Physics` holds a `PhysicsData*` at `+0x60`. **We have been reading the data object, not the physics object.** Sweeping our own pointer for `insert2` would have found nothing and we would have blamed the offsets. **Revised step 1: get from `PhysicsData` to `WheeledPhysics` first.** The verified stable vehicle back-pointer at `+0x180` is the obvious candidate -- if it is the `vehicleBaseObject`, then Report 36 SS3.4's documented `vehicleBaseObject.physics` chain completes the route, and that chain is already demonstrated readable by a shipped mod. Confirm it before anything else. **(3) THE CONFIGURATION-VERSUS-DERIVED QUESTION IS ALREADY ANSWERED, IN OUR FAVOUR.** Virtual slot `0x140` is `LoadSomeVehiclePhysicsStuff(Handle<VehicleDriveModelData_Record>*)`, annotated *"populates some variables along with most of insert1"*. **`insert1` is fed from the drive-model record**, which is the signature of configuration -- exactly what step 3 was designed to establish by a session of logging, and the opposite of `insert2`'s *WheelRuntimeData*. It also means `insert1` should hold the per-wheel `FrontPreset`/`BackPreset` values, `frictionMulLateral` among them. Step 3 still runs, but now as confirmation rather than as the deciding evidence. **Read two more headers before writing any code:** `vehiclePhysicsInsert1.hpp` (which names the fields) and `vehiclePhysicsData.hpp` (which should explain our own verified `+0x18`, `+0x180` and `+0x1BC`, and is the cheapest way to check whether those readings mean what we think). Neither was on disk. **Standing caveat, unchanged and now visible in the header itself:** every offset above is 1.52-1.6, and the file carries an `unk2_21_new[32]` inserted mid-struct by a later patch. The names carry forward; the offsets do not. |
| IDEA-504 | **ENG** | 2026-09-26 | **`WheeledPhysics::SetupInsert()` is the alternative to poking fields -- ask the engine to re-apply the drive model to a live instance instead of writing its cached copy.** Report 36 §3.4 lists it as **named in the SDK and called by no inspected mod**, the obvious candidate for re-applying a record to an already-spawned vehicle. If it is callable and safe it collapses the spawn cache that §AT was written around and §AU only sidestepped: edit the flat, call the function, and the car you are sitting in reads the new values without a respawn. | Report 36 §3.4 | **BOOKMARKED -- not scheduled** | Parts; a `KB-136` successor path; potentially a replacement for force interception entirely | **Strictly riskier than `IDEA-503` and should follow it rather than replace it.** Writing a float is reversible by writing it back; calling an engine function with hand-built arguments is not. It may allocate, may expect locks we do not hold, and runs on a body twelve threads are already touching. Two specific cautions. **(1)** LTBF's `ResetWheels()` -- the one open-source attempt at a wheel-reinit path -- is **present but commented out**, which is the residue a failed attempt leaves behind. Read why before repeating it. **(2)** This is a *record*-sourced change, so it inherits §3.3's hardest constraint unchanged: two drivers in the same car model cannot differ, because the record is shared. That is fatal for a parts economy on its own and makes this a weather or track-condition mechanism rather than a per-player one. The per-player route remains `IDEA-503`. <br><br>**AMENDED the same day: `SetupInsert()` is probably the wrong function to reach for.** Reading the header turned up a better one. **Virtual slot `0x140`, `LoadSomeVehiclePhysicsStuff(Handle<game::data::VehicleDriveModelData_Record>*)`**, annotated *"populates some variables along with most of insert1"* and **overridden by `WheeledPhysics`, `CarPhysics` and `BikePhysics`**. That is *re-apply this drive-model record to this live instance*, expressed as a single call the engine already makes at construction. It beats `SetupInsert()` on three counts: it is virtual, so it dispatches correctly for a car versus a bike without us choosing; it takes the record handle directly, so there is no hand-built argument to get wrong; and it is the function the engine itself uses for this job. `SetupInsert()` remains useful as corroboration -- its 1.6 pattern opens `8B B9 C0 05 00 00`, which is `mov edi,[rcx+0x5C0]`, i.e. it reads `insert1Count`, confirming both the layout and that it operates on `insert1`. **Neither is safe to call yet** and the two cautions above stand in full. |
| IDEA-505 | **ENG** | 2026-09-26 | **Every Part declares which channel its felt effect terminates in, and Parts ships correct even if `IDEA-503` never returns an answer.** **AMENDED the same day it was written, after reading the code: there are FOUR channels, not three.** The first draft omitted the one that actually ships, which is exactly the error the row exists to prevent. **(1) RECORD** -- a TweakDB drive-model flat, via `machine.flats` and the carrier's `effects/tweak.cpp`. **Built, shipping, and the broad one:** twenty flats across eight families. But spawn-cached, so it reaches a car at its next spawn and never the one you are sitting in, and keyed by *record*, so on one client every Galena shares one set of values. **(2) INSTANCE** -- the `gripScale` lateral write: live, reversible, per-instance, per-player (`L148`), and exactly **one quantity wide**. **(3) SIMULATED** -- whatever the server computes and shows without the car feeling different. Always safe, already server-authoritative (`IDEA-458`). **(4) PER-CORNER** -- does not exist and may never (`IDEA-503`). | §AV2 negative; owner sequencing decision `L149` | **DESIGN RULE for the Parts phase** | 18_PARTS_ECOSYSTEM; 20_WEAR_LIFECYCLE; Gate 6 composer | The failure this prevents is concrete, and Report 36 §8.1 warned about its general form: design a Brakes family around brake bias, build the economy and the UI on top of it, then discover the channel does not exist. **Tonight made that warning specific** -- bias is the exact thing §AV2 tried and failed to reach. So a Part's data carries its channel, the composer refuses to promise a felt effect on channel 3, and channel-3 parts are authored as simulated-only with a documented upgrade path. If `IDEA-503` lands they gain a physical expression without their economy, wear model or UI changing. If it never lands, nothing is owed and nothing was a lie. **Corollary:** a Part whose entire point is asymmetry -- an anti-roll bar, a brake-bias adjuster -- should not be authored in the first pass at all, because a simulated-only version of it is indistinguishable from a number with no meaning. **The amendment's real content: channels 1 and 2 have opposite strengths and neither dominates.** RECORD is broad but shared and stale; INSTANCE is live and personal but one field wide. The consequence for Parts is sharp and is now asserted in `parts.ontology`: **tyres are the only family with a per-player felt expression.** Every other family that can be felt at all is felt through the record channel, where two drivers in the same car model cannot differ from each other -- which sits directly across the premise of a parts economy and is the constraint Report 36 SS8.1 named as the hardest one. It is not fatal: parts are fitted between outings, not mid-race, so spawn-caching matches how people actually use them. But a Part that needs to make *your* Galena differ from *their* Galena has exactly one channel and it is one quantity wide. |
| IDEA-506 | **ENG** | 2026-09-26 | **Compatibility is built and derived, and the honest finding is that NCM knows one third of what it needs to know about its own cars.** `parts.architecture` and `parts.compat` implement 18_PARTS_ECOSYSTEM layers 2 and 3: fourteen mechanical facets over closed vocabularies, a per-vehicle profile, and a resolver that relates a component's constraints to a vehicle's architecture without any fitment list existing anywhere. **Coverage: 124 of 378 (vehicle, facet) pairs, 33 percent, across 27 vehicles.** | 18_PARTS_ECOSYSTEM conflict 4; `L150` | **BUILT -- 21 tests green under both interpreters** | Parts R&D, manufacturing, inventory; the component design schema | **The design decision that matters is what happens when NCM does not know.** The spec calls this *"new authoring against 27 existing catalogue entries"*, and most of it genuinely cannot be authored from the repository: **nothing here says whether a Thorton Galena is front- or rear-wheel drive.** That lives in `vehDriveModelData` in TweakDB and only a running game can read it. So every facet value carries a **provenance** -- `derived` (computed from `data.vehicle_catalogue`, true by construction), `authored` (a human said so; **none yet**), or `unknown` -- and the resolver returns **three** outcomes rather than a boolean: `fits`, `mismatch` (a real, permanent no) and `unknown_facet` (*we have not authored this*). **Collapsing the last two would have produced a system that reports an empty world and looks like it is working.** `M.authoringGaps()` returns the worklist -- currently **254 outstanding pairs** -- which is a more useful deliverable than 27 rows of plausible fiction. **What IS derived, and cost nothing:** `axle_config`, `steering` and `platform` for all 27; the full motorcycle and Formula architectures, because a bike has two wheels and handlebars *because it is a bike*, and the RB15 is a monocoque with full aero *because it is a Formula car*. **Platforms are derived from NCM's own `vehicleKey`s**: 27 vehicles collapse to **22 platforms**, with `quadra_type66`/`_nomad`, `mizutani_shion`/`_nomad`, the three Brennan Apollos and the two Yaiba Kusanagis sharing. That is what stops the component catalogue growing with every cosmetic variant, and it was free. `thorton_colby_pickup` deliberately does **not** collapse into `thorton_colby` -- a pickup is not a trim level of a coupe, and the conservative direction is to keep them apart. **The scaling requirement is a test, not a promise.** A constraint names facets and never vehicles, so `a_new_vehicle_needs_no_component_edits` asserts that every catalogue entry resolves decisively against derived facets alone. Exact model fitment stays possible through `fitsPlatforms`, kept in its own field so an audit can find every component that needed an exception. |

| IDEA-509 | **ENG** | 2026-09-26 | **The state bag has an undocumented payload size limit, now measured, and nothing guards against it.** Bisected live on 2026-09-26: **4,032 bytes is accepted, roughly 5,010 is refused** with `state bag write refused: state_value_too_large`. The old projection write was a bare assignment, so a refusal became a script error that aborted the whole function, which is why its own cleanup bookkeeping could never run either. **The deeper lesson is the boundary, not the limit:** the payload was 4x too big because it was shipping `trace`, the per-flat audit trail, to every client.
